\chapter{Preface}\label{preface}

\section{Acknowledgments}\label{acknowledgments}

PyPedal was initially written to support the author's dissertation
research while at \href{http://www.lsu.edu/}{Louisiana State
University}. The initial development was supported in part by a grant
from The Seeing Eye, Inc. (Morristown, NJ). The author wishes to thank
Paul VanRaden for many helpful suggestions for improving the ability of
PyPedal to handle computations in large pedigrees. Additional feedback
in the form of bug reports, feature requests, and discussion of
computing strategies was provided by Dan Cieslak, Bradley J. Heins
(University of Minnesota), Edward H. Hagen (Institute for Theoretical
Biology, Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin), Kathy Hanford (University of
Nebraska), Thomas Kelly (Department of Animal and Poultry Science,
University of Guelph), Thomas von Hassell, Gianluca Saba, and Ken
Severin (University of Alaska -- Fairbanks). Gregor Gorjanc wrote a blog
entry describing
\href{http://ggorjan.blogspot.com/2007/04/installing-pypedal-under-debian.html}{how
to install PyPedal on Debian Linux}. Fernando Perez posted a LaTeX
preamble to the NumPy listserver that dramatically improved the PDF
version of this manual.

The Newfoundland pedigree was taken from the
\href{http://www.newfoundlanddog-database.net/en/}{NewFoundland Dog
database} and is used with permission.

The pedigree of European royalty used in the GEDCOM discussion,
\href{http://www.genealogyforum.com/gedcom/gedcom1/ged3.htm}{ged3.ged},
was taken from the \href{http://www.genealogyforum.com/}{Genealogy Forum
website}. It is believed to be in the public domain, and is used with
the knowledge of the website administrators.

\chapter{NAME: introduction.qmd}\label{name-introduction.qmd}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, bottomrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, colback=white, left=2mm, breakable, rightrule=.15mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, arc=.35mm]

Any sufficiently disguised bug is indistinguishable from a feature. ---
Rich Kulawiec

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{What is PyPedal?}\label{what-is-pypedal}

This chapter introduces PyPedal, a Python-language tool for working with
pedigrees.

PyPedal (\textbf{Py}thon \textbf{Ped}igree An\textbf{al}ysis) is a tool
for analyzing pedigree files. It calculates several quantitative
measures of genetic diversity from pedigrees, including average
coefficients of inbreeding and relationship, effective founder numbers,
and effective ancestor numbers. Checks are performed catch common
mistakes in pedigree files, such as parents with more recent birthdates
or smaller ID numbers than their offspring and animals appearing as both
sires and dams in the pedigree. Tools for pedigree visualization and
report generation are also provided. PyPedal only makes use of
information on pedigree structure, not individual genotypes. Genotypes
can be assigned to founders for use in gene-dropping simulations to
calculate the effective number of founder genomes.

PyPedal is a \href{http://www.python.org/}{Python} language module that
may be called by programs or used interactively. You must have Python
3.11 (or later) installed in order to use PyPedal. The NumPy module must
also be installed in order for you to use PyPedal. In addition, there
are several third-party packages used by PyPedal, which are discussed in
the chapter on installing PyPedal.

This manual is the official documentation for PyPedal. It includes a
tutorial and is the most authoritative source of information about
PyPedal with the exception of the source code. The tutorial material
will walk you through a series of manipulations of a simple pedigree.
All users of PyPedal are encouraged to follow the tutorial with a
working installation.

\section{Features}\label{features}

Key features of PyPedal include:

\begin{itemize}
\tightlist
\item
  Reading pedigree files in user-defined formats;
\item
  Checking pedigree integrity (duplicate IDs, parents younger than
  offspring, etc.);
\item
  Generating summary information such as frequency of appearance in the
  pedigree file;
\item
  Reordering and renumbering of pedigree files;
\item
  Computation of the numerator relationship matrix (\(A\)) from a
  pedigree file using the tabular method;
\item
  Inbreeding and relationship calculations for large pedigrees
  (pedigrees up to 600,000 animals have been processed with PyPedal);
\item
  Computation of coefficients of inbreeding using the Meuwissen and Luo
  (1992) and modified Meuwissen and Luo (Quaas, 1995, unpublished)
  algorithms;
\item
  Computation of average total and individual coefficients of inbreeding
  and relationship;
\item
  Calculation of coefficients of partial inbreeding using an iterative
  tabular method (Lacy, Alaks, and Walsh 1996; Gulisija et al. 2006);
\item
  Calculation of coefficients of ancestral inbreeding using the methods
  of Ballou (1997) or Suwanlee et al. (2007);
\item
  Decomposition of \(A\) into \(T\) and \(D\) such that \(A=TDT'\);
\item
  Computation of the direct inverse of \(A\) (not accounting for
  inbreeding) using the method of Henderson (1976);
\item
  Computation of the direct inverse of \(A\) (accounting for inbreeding)
  using the method of Quaas (1976);
\item
  Storage of \(A\) and its inverse between user sessions as persistent
  Python objects using the \texttt{pickle} module to avoid unnecessary
  calculations;
\item
  Calculation of theoretical and actual effective population sizes;
\item
  Computation of effective founder number using the exact algorithm of
  Lacy (1989);
\item
  Computation of effective founder number using the approximate
  algorithm of Boichard et al. (1997);
\item
  Computation of effective ancestor number using the algorithms of
  Boichard et al. (1997);
\item
  Selection of subpedigrees containing all ancestors of an animal;
\item
  Identification of the common relatives of two animals;
\item
  Calculation of the inbreeding of offspring from a prospective mating;
\item
  Output to ASCII text files, including matrices, coefficients of
  inbreeding and relationship, and summary information;
\item
  Importation and exportation of GEDCOM 5.5 files;
\item
  Importation and exportation of GENES 1.20 (dBase III) files;
\item
  Loading pedigrees from, and saving them to, MySQL, Postgres, and
  SQLite databases;
\item
  Simulation of pedigrees using an algorithm derived from that used in
  Matvec 1.1a;
\item
  Set operations on pedigrees (unions and intersections);
\end{itemize}

PyPedal has been used to perform calculations on pedigrees as large as
600,000 animals and has been used in scientific research (Cole, Franke,
and Leighton 2004; Cole 2007).

\section{Where to get information and
code}\label{where-to-get-information-and-code}

Code and documentation are available from the
\href{https://github.com/wintermind/pypedal3}{PyPedal 3 Github
repository}.

\chapter{NAME: input-output.qmd}\label{name-input-output.qmd}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, bottomrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, colback=white, left=2mm, breakable, rightrule=.15mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, arc=.35mm]

The only legitimate use of a computer is to play games. --- Eugene
Jarvis

\end{tcolorbox}

Getting data into and out of programs, while extremely important to
end-users, is often challenging. PyPedal can load pedigrees from --- and
save them to --- many different sources. A list of supported input and
output formats is shown in Table Table~\ref{tbl-input-output-methods}.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1130}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1043}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.7826}}@{}}
\caption{PyPedal input and output
methods.}\label{tbl-input-output-methods}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Direction
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Source
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Description
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Direction
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Source
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright

\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
Input & db & Load an ASDx-formatted data from an SQLite database \\
& fromgraph & Load a pedigree from an instance of an XDiGraph object
(not a file) \\
& gedcomfile & Load a pedigree from a GEDCOM 5.5 file \\
& graphfile & Load a pedigree from an adjacency list using the
\texttt{read\_adjlist()} function from NetworkX \\
& simulate & Simulate a pedigree rathen that loading it from a file \\
& textstream & Load a pedigree from a string containing animal
records \\
Output & save & Save a pedigree with user-specified filenames, pedigree
format codes, and separators \\
& savedb & Save a pedigree to a database table \\
& savegedcom & Save a pedigree to a GEDCOM 5.5 file \\
& savegedcom & Save a pedigree to a GENES 1.20 file (DBASE III
format) \\
& savegraph & Save a pedigree to a file as an adjacency list \\
\end{longtable}

It has evolved over time that the input methods are implemented as cases
of \texttt{pedsource} in the \texttt{NewPedigree::load()} method, while
output methods are implemented as individual methods of
\texttt{NewPedigree}. While it is quite straightforward to add new
methods for pedigree output, it is trickier to add new input sources.
The greater difficulty in adding new input sources is largely due to the
mysterious workings of the \texttt{NewPedigree::preprocess()} method,
which walks through input line-by-line to load the pedigree and perform
a number of integrity checks. Once the data are loaded into a
\texttt{NewPedigree} object it's easy to output them because there's no
need to check the integrity of the pedigree and relationships among the
records in the pedigree. If you want to implement a new input source the
sanity-saving way to go is to get the data into a list that you can pass
to \texttt{preprocess()}; \texttt{preprocess()} can then walk the list
as it would walk through the lines of an input file.

Database operations are discussed in their own section at the end of
this chapter.

\section{Input}\label{input}

The process of data input has been made as simple as is reasonably
possible, but it is ultimately the responsibility of the user to prepare
their data. Most people will load their data from a simple text file,
with one pedigree entry per line. A number of data integrity checks are
performed when a pedigree is loaded. First, the pedigree format string
is checked for validity and compared to a line of data to make sure that
the specfied number of columns of data exist. Once that check is passed,
individual records are loaded and checked one at a time. As individual
records are validated they're added to the pedigree. Duplicate records
are eliminated, parents that appear in the pedigree but do not have
their own record in the input data are added to the pedigree, and sexes
are checked (animals cannot appear as both males and females). If animal
IDs are strings they're hashed to get numeric IDs for use in reordering
and renumbering.

If you used an earlier version of PyPedal you may have added a pedigree
format string to your pedigree file. You don't need to include that
string in the current version of PyPedal, and if
\texttt{NewPedigree::preprocess()} sees one while reading a pedigree
file it will ignore it. You may include comment lines in your file as
well; they will also be ignored.

\subsection{Graph Objects}\label{graph-objects}

Pedigrees can be represented as a type of mathematical object called a
directed graph (digraph; not to be confused with visualizations of
data). The \texttt{NetworkX} module provides Python tools for working
with digraphs, and PyPedal provides a convenient way for loading a
pedigree from an instance of an \texttt{XDiGraph} object.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{example }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(optionsfile}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}new\_networkx.ini\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{ng }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_network.ped\_to\_graph(example)}
\NormalTok{options }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{\}}
\NormalTok{options[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedfile\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}dummy\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{options[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedformat\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}asd\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{example2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(options,pedsource}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}graph\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,pedgraph}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ng)}
\NormalTok{example2.metadata.printme()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can see from the printout of the metadata from the new pedigree,
\texttt{example2}, that the graph \texttt{ng} was successfully converted
to a \texttt{NewPedigree} object.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Metadata }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{  Testing fromgraph() (dummy)}
\NormalTok{Records:                }\DecValTok{13}
\NormalTok{Unique Sires:           }\DecValTok{3}
\NormalTok{Unique Dams:            }\DecValTok{4}
\NormalTok{Unique Gens:            }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{Unique Years:           }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{Unique Founders:        }\DecValTok{5}
\NormalTok{Unique Herds:           }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{Pedigree Code:          asd}
\NormalTok{\textbackslash{}end\{verbatim\}}
\CommentTok{\textquotesingle{}\textquotesingle{}\textquotesingle{}}

\AlertTok{\#\#\#}\CommentTok{ GEDCOM Files}

\CommentTok{A thorough description of support for GEDCOM files may be found in the Appendix.}

\AlertTok{\#\#\#}\CommentTok{ GENES Files}

\CommentTok{A thorough description of support for GENES files may be found in the Appendix.}

\AlertTok{\#\#\#}\CommentTok{ Text Files}

\CommentTok{Most users will load their pedigrees from simple text files. As an example, consider a large dog pedigree, an excerpt of}
\CommentTok{which is presented below.}

\CommentTok{\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\textasciigrave{}\{python\}}
\CommentTok{\#| eval: false      \# Don\textquotesingle{}t evaluate this chunk}
\CommentTok{\#| echo: true       \# Do show this chunk in the final rendered document}
\CommentTok{\# dogID,fatherID,motherID,gender,born}
\CommentTok{64 66 67 2 1979}
\CommentTok{63 64 65 1 1982}
\CommentTok{62 191 195 2 1982}
\CommentTok{61 64 65 2 1982}
\CommentTok{...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This pedigree can be loaded using this short program:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{\textbackslash{}begin\{verbatim\}}
\NormalTok{options }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedfile\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}dog.ped\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedname\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}A Large Dog Pedigree\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedformat\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}asdgb\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,}
\NormalTok{\}}
\ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{\_\_name\_\_} \OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}\_\_main\_\_\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    test }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(options)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

There are examples of loading pedigrees from text files throughout this
manual.

\subsection{Text Streams}\label{text-streams}

There are some use cases, such as web services, for which it may be
desirable to load pedigrees from strings rather tham from files. This is
done by passing the \texttt{pedsource} keyword to
\texttt{pyp\_newclasses.load\_pedigree()} with a value of
\texttt{textstream}, along with a string named `pedstream':

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{options }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{\}}
\NormalTok{options[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedfile\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{options[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}messages\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}verbose\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{options[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedformat\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}ASD\textquotesingle{}}

\ControlFlowTok{if} \VariableTok{\_\_name\_\_} \OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{"\_\_main\_\_"}\NormalTok{:}
\NormalTok{    pedstream }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}a1,s1,d1}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{a2,s2,d2}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{a3,a1,a2}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{    test }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(options, pedsource}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}textstream\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, pedstream}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{pedstream)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Only ASD-formatted pedigrees can be loaded this way, individual IDs are
separated with commas, and successive records are separated by newlines.
These restrictions are hard-coded into the \texttt{NewPedigree::load()}
method. All records must contain a newline, including the last record in
the string! You must also set the \texttt{pedfile} option to some value,
even if that value is just an empty string, as in the example above.

The expected use case is that the pedigree would be retrieved from a
database, and the result set from the SQL query converted into a string.
There's an upper bound on the size of pedigree that can be loaded from a
stream based on the physical memory available on your platform, and
extremely large pedigrees should be loaded from a text file.

\section{Output}\label{output}

\subsection{GEDCOM Files}\label{gedcom-files}

A thorough description of GEDCOM file output may be found in the
Appendix.

\subsection{GENES Files}\label{genes-files}

A thorough description of GENES file output may be found in the
Appendix.

\subsection{Graph Objects}\label{graph-objects-1}

\texttt{NewPedigree::savegraph()} saves a pedigree to a file as an
adjacency list, which is a commonly-used format for describing digraphs.
If the pedigree has already been converted to a digraph, pass it using
the \texttt{pedgraph} argument; otherwise, the method will call the
appropriate converter:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test.savegraph(pedoutfile}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}test.adj\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The file \texttt{test.adj} has the following contents:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# sqlite.py}
\CommentTok{\# GMT Tue Mar  4 20:38:52 2008}
\CommentTok{\# Text Stream}
\DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{5}
\DecValTok{2} \DecValTok{6}
\DecValTok{3} \DecValTok{5}
\DecValTok{4} \DecValTok{6}
\DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{7}
\DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{7}
\DecValTok{7}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This example shows there can be a considerable loss of information when
going from a PyPedal pedigree to some other way of representing the
pedigree, such as a graph.

\subsection{Text Files}\label{text-files}

The \texttt{NewPedigree::save()} method writes a PyPedal pedigree to a
user-specified file based on a pedigree format string and optional
column separator provided by the user. By default, the pedformat is
\texttt{asd} and the sepchar is \texttt{}. Any attribute of a
\texttt{NewAnimal} object can be written to an output file.

\texttt{NewPedigree::save()} tries to never overwrite your data. If you
don't pass a filename argument a file whose name is derived from, but
not the same as, the original pedigree filename will be used. The string
\texttt{\_saved} will be appended to the filename in order to
distinguish it from the original pedigree file.

There are a few points that you need to be aware of when using
\texttt{NewPedigree::save()}. First, you may use any column separator
you choose \textbf{except} the empty string
(\texttt{sepchar=\textquotesingle{}\textquotesingle{}}); if you try and
do that the sepchar will be changed to the global default (currently
\texttt{}), and a message written to the log. This means there's no way
for you to run all the columns in your pedigree file together such that
they cannot easily be separated. Sorry. You can use anoy other string
you like to separate your columns (spaces, columns, and tabs have been
tested). It \textbf{is} possible to write an incomplete pedigree file,
that is, a file which does not include animal, sire, and dam
information. PyPedal checks the pedformat to see if it contains either
\texttt{asd} or \texttt{ASD} and warns you if it doesn't, but it'll
still write the file you tell it to. The burden is on you, the user, to
make sure your output file contains all the information you want it to.
Finally, you can write whatever attribute you like to whichever column
you like in the output file. For the sake of your sanity I strongly
recommend that you always place the animal, sire, and dam IDs in the
first three columns, but you're an adult and may do what you like.

Maybe a couple of examples will help clear that up. Here's the simplest
case, taking defaults:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test.save(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}test\_save\_asd.ped\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

gets you the result:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# test\_save\_asd.ped created by PyPedal at Tue Sep 28 16:39:36 2010}
\CommentTok{\# Current pedigree metadata:}
\CommentTok{\#       pedigree file: test\_save\_asd.ped}
\CommentTok{\#       pedigree name: Untitled}
\CommentTok{\#       pedigree format: asd}
\CommentTok{\#       }\AlertTok{NOTE}\CommentTok{: Animal, sire, and dam IDs are RENUMBERED IDs, not original IDs!}
\CommentTok{\# Original pedigree metadata:}
\CommentTok{\#       pedigree file: ../simulated\_pedigree.ped}
\CommentTok{\#       pedigree name: Untitled}
\CommentTok{\#       pedigree format: asdxg}
\DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{2} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{3} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{4} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{...}
\DecValTok{21} \DecValTok{19} \DecValTok{10}
\DecValTok{22} \DecValTok{9} \DecValTok{12}
\DecValTok{23} \DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{10}
\DecValTok{24} \DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{21}
\DecValTok{25} \DecValTok{13} \DecValTok{8}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Note the friendly header to tell you what you've got and where it came
from. The note about renumbered IDs is particularly important -- if the
\texttt{pedigree\_is\_renumbered} flag is set then you will get this
message to alert you that comparing this file against your original
source file may give confusing results. They're not really conflicting,
but if you compare renumbered with original IDs then you may assume
there's some kind of problem when really there is not. Original and
renumbered pedigrees are mathematically equivalent assuming there were
no errors in the original file that were corrected by PyPedal. Isn't
that reassuring? From here on out I will not show the header, and will
include only the first five rows of data in the file for the sake of
brevity (like I've ever let that stop me before). You can get the same
thing as a CSV using the sepchar argument:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test.save(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}test\_save\_asd\_csv.ped\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, sepchar}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{},\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

which produces:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{,}\DecValTok{0}
\NormalTok{...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Sometimes you want to go all the way and dump everything into a file.
This is like one of those pizzas with every variety of cured meat known
to food science piled up on top. Here you go:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test.save(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}test\_save\_combo\_all.ped\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, pedformat}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}asdgxbfrnylepASDLhHu\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

which gives you what you asked for, even if many of the values aren't
particularly interesting:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ m }\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{1011900} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Breed }\DecValTok{7} \DecValTok{1900} \DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{999} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{999.0} \DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Name Unknown\_Name [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000071\_\_1\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000071\_\_2\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\DecValTok{57361}\ErrorTok{b5fd9993f00437fbe4c4675feca}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Herd}
\DecValTok{2} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ m }\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{1011900} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Breed }\DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{1900} \DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{999} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{999.0} \DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Name Unknown\_Name [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000061\_\_1\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000061\_\_2\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\DecValTok{57361}\ErrorTok{b5fd9993f00437fbe4c4675feca}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Herd}
\DecValTok{3} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ m }\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{1011900} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Breed }\DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{1900} \DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{999} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{999.0} \DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Name Unknown\_Name [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000051\_\_1\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000051\_\_2\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\DecValTok{57361}\ErrorTok{b5fd9993f00437fbe4c4675feca}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Herd}
\DecValTok{4} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ f }\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{1011900} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Breed }\DecValTok{3} \DecValTok{1900} \DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{999} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{999.0} \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Name Unknown\_Name [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000031\_\_1\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000031\_\_2\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\DecValTok{57361}\ErrorTok{b5fd9993f00437fbe4c4675feca}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Herd}
\DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{ f }\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{1011900} \FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Breed }\DecValTok{2} \DecValTok{1900} \DecValTok{0} \OperatorTok{{-}}\DecValTok{999} \OperatorTok{{-}}\FloatTok{999.0} \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Name Unknown\_Name [}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000021\_\_1\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1900000000000000021\_\_2\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\DecValTok{57361}\ErrorTok{b5fd9993f00437fbe4c4675feca}\NormalTok{ Unknown\_Herd}
\NormalTok{...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Okay, I get it. You're like the Nigel Tufnel of Missouri -- you want to
see something that goes to 11. I pondered this for a while. You've read
this far and deserve to be rewarded. So I tried to think of the most
awesomest thing that I could think of. So here you go, LSU fans,
straight from Death Valley by way of Beltsville, MD:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test.save(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}test\_save\_peterson.ped\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, pedformat}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}asd\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, sepchar}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}...Kneel before Zod!...\textquotesingle{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Maybe Patrick Peterson is really into genealogy -- you never know --
stop looking at me like that! This is a perfectly cromulent example of
the extremes to which the sepchar argument can be pushed:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{..Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}\NormalTok{...Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}
\FloatTok{2.}\NormalTok{..Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}\NormalTok{...Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}
\FloatTok{3.}\NormalTok{..Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}\NormalTok{...Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}
\FloatTok{4.}\NormalTok{..Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}\NormalTok{...Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}
\FloatTok{5.}\NormalTok{..Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}\NormalTok{...Kneel before Zod}\OperatorTok{!}\NormalTok{..}\FloatTok{.0}
\NormalTok{...}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You're thinking, ``My eyes! Why would he do that?!'' The same reason Zod
gave us a little Heisman pose in the end zone after returning a West
Virginia kickoff for 6 -- because I can. (This kind of writing is
probably going to get me in trouble one day, but the boss is out of town
and there's no one here to rein-in my behavior!) Wait, though. It gets
better. PyPedal can get all Ourboros on this unholy creation:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# But, wait, can this actually work?}
\NormalTok{options2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ options}
\NormalTok{options2[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedfile\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}test\_save\_peterson.ped\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{options2[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}sepchar\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}...Kneel before Zod!...\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{options2[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedformat\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}asd\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{test2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(options2)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

When you run this bit of code you see that it poses no problem at all
for PyPedal. Perhaps political dissidents can communicate secretly by
doing strange things with \texttt{sepchar}, although it's more likely
that PhD students will find a clever way to abuse MS students with it,
instead.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{[INFO]: Logfile untitled\_pedigree.log instantiated.}
\NormalTok{[INFO]: Preprocessing test\_save\_peterson.ped}
\NormalTok{[INFO]: Opening pedigree }\BuiltInTok{file}\NormalTok{ test\_save\_peterson.ped}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]: Renumbering pedigree at Wed Sep }\DecValTok{29} \DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{5} \DecValTok{2010}
\NormalTok{                [INFO]: Reordering pedigree at Wed Sep }\DecValTok{29} \DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{5} \DecValTok{2010}
\NormalTok{                [INFO]: Renumbering at Wed Sep }\DecValTok{29} \DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{5} \DecValTok{2010}
\NormalTok{                [INFO]: Updating ID }\BuiltInTok{map}\NormalTok{ at Wed Sep }\DecValTok{29} \DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{5} \DecValTok{2010}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]: Assigning offspring at Wed Sep }\DecValTok{29} \DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{42}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{0}\ErrorTok{5} \DecValTok{2010}
\NormalTok{[INFO]: Creating pedigree metadata }\BuiltInTok{object}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Instantiating a new PedigreeMetadata() }\BuiltInTok{object}\NormalTok{...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Naming the Pedigree()...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Assigning a filename...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Attaching a pedigree...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Setting the pedcode...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Counting the number of animals }\KeywordTok{in}\NormalTok{ the pedigree...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Counting }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ finding unique sires...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Counting }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ finding unique dams...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Setting renumbered flag...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Counting }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ finding unique generations...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Counting }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ finding unique birthyears...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Counting }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ finding unique founders...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Counting }\KeywordTok{and}\NormalTok{ finding unique herds...}
\NormalTok{        [INFO]:  Detaching pedigree...}
\NormalTok{Metadata }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{ Untitled (test\_save\_peterson.ped)}
\NormalTok{        Records:                }\DecValTok{25}
\NormalTok{        Unique Sires:           }\DecValTok{7}
\NormalTok{        Unique Dams:            }\DecValTok{7}
\NormalTok{        Unique Gens:            }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        Unique Years:           }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        Unique Founders:        }\DecValTok{6}
\NormalTok{        Unique Herds:           }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{        Pedigree Code:          asd}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

How's that for the strangest thing you're likely to see all day? I'm not
suggesting that you seriously use column separators the way I just did,
but this example shows that you can do some unusual things with PyPedal,
and perhaps you'll come up with something very useful because of that
flexibility. If nothing else, you now have a greater appreciation of
what you can do with a dynamic language and questionable processing of
user inputs. Remember, never trust any data a user gives you. They're a
shifty lot, users.

\subsection{Text Streams}\label{text-streams-1}

\texttt{NewPedigree::tostream()} returns a text stream from an instance
of a \texttt{NewPedigree} object. The text stream contains an
ASD-formatted pedigree as a string in which individual IDs are separated
by commas and successive records are separated by newlines.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ pedstream }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}a1,s1,d1}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{a2,s2,d2}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{a3,a1,a2}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ test }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(options,pedsource}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}textstream\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{,pedstream}\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{pedstream)}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ pedstream2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ test.tostream()}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}} \BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{ pedstream2}
\CommentTok{\textquotesingle{}d1,0,0}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\CommentTok{d2,0,0}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\CommentTok{s1,0,0}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\CommentTok{s2,0,0}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\CommentTok{a2,s2,d2}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\CommentTok{a1,s1,d1}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\CommentTok{a3,a1,a2}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}n}\CommentTok{\textquotesingle{}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Note that in this example the input and output text streams differ
because the input stream did not include pedigree entries for all
animals in the pedigree (for example, sire \texttt{s1}). Recall that
PyPedal adds missing entries for parents when the pedigree is loaded.

\subsection{Databases}\label{databases}

PyPedal is able to load pedigrees from, and save them to, three
databases: MySQL, Postgres, and SQLite. PyPedal uses the pyDAL database
abstraction library (https://github.com/web2py/pydal) to talk to these
databases. Additional databases are supported by pyDAL and require only
minor changes to the connection strings in the
\texttt{connectToDatabase} function in the \texttt{pyp\_db} module in
order to work with PyPedal as well.

A list of the database-related keywords may be found in Table
Table~\ref{tbl-database-parameters}.

\begin{longtable}[]{@{}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1478}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.1130}}
  >{\raggedright\arraybackslash}p{(\linewidth - 4\tabcolsep) * \real{0.7391}}@{}}
\caption{Database access parameters and their default
values.}\label{tbl-database-parameters}\tabularnewline
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Parameter
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Default
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Description
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endfirsthead
\toprule\noalign{}
\begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Parameter
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Default
\end{minipage} & \begin{minipage}[b]{\linewidth}\raggedright
Description
\end{minipage} \\
\midrule\noalign{}
\endhead
\bottomrule\noalign{}
\endlastfoot
database\_debug & False & Toggle debugging messages in the database
module on and off. \\
database\_host & `localhost' & The server on which the database is
running. \\
database\_name & `pypedal' & The name of the database to be used. \\
database\_passwd & `anonymous' & The password needed to connect to your
database. \\
database\_port & '\,' & The port on which your databse is listening for
connections. \\
database\_table & `filetag' & The name of the database table to which
the current pedigree will be written. \\
database\_type & `sqlite' & The type of database you are using
(`mysql' \\
database\_user & `anonymous' & The name of a user with access rights to
your database. \\
\end{longtable}

\subsection{Input from Databases}\label{input-from-databases}

PyPedal can load \texttt{ASDx}-formatted pedigrees from an SQLite
database using
\texttt{pedsource=\textquotesingle{}db\textquotesingle{}}. The pedigree
will be loaded from the database and table specified in the
\texttt{database\_name} and \texttt{dbtable\_name} variables. Consider
the following example:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(options,pedsource}\OperatorTok{=}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}db\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{test.metadata.printme()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

This produces the output:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Metadata }\ControlFlowTok{for}\NormalTok{  DB Stream ()}
\NormalTok{    Records:                }\DecValTok{7}
\NormalTok{    Unique Sires:           }\DecValTok{3}
\NormalTok{    Unique Dams:            }\DecValTok{3}
\NormalTok{    Unique Gens:            }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    Unique Years:           }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    Unique Founders:        }\DecValTok{4}
\NormalTok{    Unique Herds:           }\DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{    Pedigree Code:          ASDx}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Note that user-supplied values of the pedigree format string will be
over-written by the \texttt{load()} method and don't affect database
processing. Database importation is hard-coded to accept only pedigrees
in that format.

It's possible to load pedigrees from a databse with a format different
than that exected by PyPedal, but it requires that you make changes to
the \texttt{NewPedigree} class's \texttt{load()} method. Consider the
database-loading code that's located at about line 826 of
\texttt{pyp\_newclasses.py}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\ControlFlowTok{elif}\NormalTok{ pedsource }\OperatorTok{==} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}db\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{:}
    \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kw[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}pedformat\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}ASDx\textquotesingle{}}
    \VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kw[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}sepchar\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{},\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{...}
    \ControlFlowTok{try}\NormalTok{:}
        \CommentTok{\# Connect to the database}
        \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ pyp\_db.does\_table\_exist(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{):}
\NormalTok{          conn }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_db.connect\_to\_database(}\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{)}
            \ControlFlowTok{if}\NormalTok{ conn:}
\NormalTok{                sql }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}SELECT * FROM }\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ ( }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kw[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}database\_table\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] )}
\NormalTok{                dbstream }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ conn.GetAll(sql)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

In order to load data from your own database you must change the
pedigree format string to match the pedigree you want to load (see Table
tbl-pedigree-format-codes for a list of pedigree format codes). You then
need to change the SQL statement to match the column names in your
database and the order of fields in your pedigree format string. The
animal, sire, and dam IDs, respectively, should always be the first
three columns in your records. The query results are stored as a list of
tuples in \texttt{dbstream} and will be unpacked in the
\texttt{preprocess()} method.

Please note that I'll try and answer questions about the way in which
PyPedal handles loading pedigrees from databases, I cannot answer
detailed questions about databases to which I don't have access. You
should carefully test your custom queries in SQL to ensure they're
working correctly before adding them to PyPedal. Also, please check your
pedigree format string to make sure it matches your query.

\subsection{Output to Databases}\label{output-to-databases}

PyPedal can write \texttt{NewPedigree} pedigrees to ASDx-formatted
SQLite tables using the \texttt{NewPedigree::savedb()} method. The
following program will result in the creation of a table named
\texttt{test\_save} in the database \texttt{test\_pypedal\_save}.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test.kw[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}database\_name\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}test\_pypedal\_save\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{test.kw[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}dbtable\_name\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}test\_save\textquotesingle{}}
\NormalTok{test.savedb()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The pedigree is saved to the database and table specified in the
\texttt{database\_name} and \texttt{dbtable\_name} variables. If the
specified table already exists then the records from the current
pedigree will be added to the table. If you want to overwrite the
existing database then you need to pass \texttt{drop=True} in the call
to \texttt{savedb()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{test.savedb(drop}\OperatorTok{=}\VariableTok{True}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

While this may strike some users as unexpected behavior it is based on
the idea that you should never lose data unexpectedly. If you use only
the defaults you'll never lose data. However, if you try and save a
renumbered pedigree on top of another renumbered pedigree you'll run
into problems because animal IDs are used as primary keys, and you'll
have constraint violations.

It's possible to save pedigrees to a databse with a format different
than that exected by PyPedal, but it's a little harder than changeing
pedigree input. Changing the output table requires that you change the
\texttt{NewPedigree} class's \texttt{savedb} method. Consider the code
that begins on or about line 1412 of \texttt{pyp\_newclasses.py} in
\texttt{savedb}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sql }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}create table }\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{ ( }\OperatorTok{\textbackslash{}}
\StringTok{    animalName   varchar(128) primary key, }\OperatorTok{\textbackslash{}}
\StringTok{    sireName     varchar(128), }\OperatorTok{\textbackslash{}}
\StringTok{    damName      varchar(128), }\OperatorTok{\textbackslash{}}
\StringTok{    sex          char(1) }\OperatorTok{\textbackslash{}}
\StringTok{    );\textquotesingle{}} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ ( }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kw[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}database\_table\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You need to rewrite this statement to add or remove columns; the SQL for
a table that includes all fields in a \texttt{NewAnimal} object may be
found in the \texttt{create\_pedigree\_table} function of the
\texttt{pyp\_db} module. Note that not every field in a
\texttt{NewAnimal} object has a corresponding pedigree format code.
Consult Table tbl-pedigree-format-codes\} for appropriate codes.

Once you've updated the SQL used to create your custom pedigree table
you'll also need to change the code which prepares the input for writing
to the database. This code is found on or about about line 1443 in
\texttt{savedb}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{sql }\OperatorTok{=} \StringTok{"INSERT INTO }\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{ ( animalName, sireName, damName, sex ) VALUES (\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}, \textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{})"} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ ( }\VariableTok{self}\NormalTok{.kw[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}database\_table\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{], an, si, da, p.sex )}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Note that you'll need to make appropriate casts yourself. For example,
you'll want to cast things that are stored as integers or reals to make
sure that you don't violate a column constraint. You must enclose
strings in single-quotation marks ('\,') yourself.

By default PyPedal saves pedigrees in \texttt{ASD} format. If you want
to save your pedigree in \texttt{asd} format then you'll need to change
the code on or about line 1436 from:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{an }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p.name}
\NormalTok{si }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p.sireName}
\NormalTok{da }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p.damName}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

to:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{an }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p.animalID}
\NormalTok{si }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p.sireID}
\NormalTok{da }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ p.damID}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

It's better to add a new method supporting your output format to
\texttt{NewAnimal} rather than changing the \texttt{savedb} method.

\bookmarksetup{startatroot}

\chapter{NAME: calculations.qmd}\label{name-calculations.qmd}

\begin{tcolorbox}[enhanced jigsaw, colframe=quarto-callout-tip-color-frame, bottomrule=.15mm, toprule=.15mm, colback=white, left=2mm, breakable, rightrule=.15mm, leftrule=.75mm, opacityback=0, arc=.35mm]

Are you quite sure that all those bells and whistles, all those
wonderful facilities of your so called powerful programming languages,
belong to the solution set rather than the problem set? --- Edsger
Dijkstra

\end{tcolorbox}

\section{Overview}\label{sec-computing-overview}

In \textbf{?@sec-inputoutput} you learned how to get your pedigree data
loaded into PyPedal. This chapter will show you what you can do with the
pedigree once it's loaded. The examples in this chapter assume that
you're working with a reordered and renumbered pedigree (see
Section~\ref{sec-methodology-reordering-and-renumbering} for additional
details).

In order to get the most out of your pedigrees you need to understand
the basic structure of a PyPedal pedigree, which consists of two
components: the pedigree itself, which is composed of a list of
\texttt{NewAnimal} objects, and metadata, which is information about the
pedigree itself. Some calculations are performed on the animal records
directly, while others use the metadata, or some combination of the two.
The fundamental goal of PyPedal is to provide the user with tools for
asking questions about their pedigrees.

The following discussion will use the following pedigree taken from
Boichard et al. (1997):

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# pedformat: asdg}
\DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{1}
\DecValTok{2} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{1}
\DecValTok{3} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{1}
\DecValTok{4} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{1}
\DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{2} \DecValTok{2}
\DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3} \DecValTok{4} \DecValTok{2}
\DecValTok{7} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3}
\DecValTok{8} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3}
\DecValTok{9} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3}
\DecValTok{10} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3}
\DecValTok{11} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3}
\DecValTok{12} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3}
\DecValTok{13} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3}
\DecValTok{14} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{3}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Many of the subsequent code snippets are taken from the
\texttt{new\_methods.py} example program.

\section{Inbreeding and Relationships}\label{sec-computing-inbreeding}

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{inbr }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_nrm.inbreeding(example)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}inbr: \textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ inbr:  \{}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}fx\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{,}
        \DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{\},}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}metadata\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}nonzero\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_max\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_avg\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_rng\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{,}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_sum\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_min\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_count\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{\},}
        \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}all\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_max\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_avg\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_rng\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_sum\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{,}
            \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_min\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}f\_count\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{\}}
\NormalTok{        \}}
\NormalTok{    \}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The dictionary returned by \texttt{inbreeding()} contains two
dictionaries: \texttt{fx} contains coefficients of inbreeding keyed to
animal IDs, and \texttt{metadata} contains summary information about the
coefficients of inbreeding in the pedigree. \texttt{metadata} also
contains two dictionaries: \texttt{nonzero} contains summary statistics
only for animals with non-zero coefficients of inbreeding, while
\texttt{all} contains statistics for all animals.

Relationship metadata, similar to the inbreeding metadata described
above but for coefficients of relationship, are available but not
calculated by default.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{inbr, reln }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_nrm.inbreeding(example,rels}\OperatorTok{=}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}reln: \textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, reln)}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ reln:  \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r\_nonzero\_count\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r\_nonzero\_avg\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.40000000000000002}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r\_min\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r\_sum\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{4.0}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r\_avg\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.19047619047619047}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r\_max\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{,}
    \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r\_count\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{21}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}r\_rng\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The dictionary of relationship metadata returned by
\texttt{inbreeding()} also contains statistics for zero and non-zero
coefficients of relationship. In the example presented above the
pedigree contains\ldots{}

Relationship metadata are not guaranteed to be correct when
\texttt{method\ =\ \textquotesingle{}vanraden\textquotesingle{}} is
used. That's because \texttt{inbreeding\_vanraden()} uses a speed-up
when there are full-sibs in the pedigree to avoid repeating
calculations. The metadata should be reasonably accurate for pedigrees
with few or no full-sibs. The summary statistics will not be very
accurate in the case of pedigrees that contain many full-sibs.

The relationship metadata don't include individual pairwise
relationships. In order to associate those with your pedigree you must
create a \texttt{NewAMatrix} object, form the numerator relationship
matrix (NRM), and attach it to the pedigree:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{options }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{\}}
\NormalTok{...}
\NormalTok{example }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(options)}
\NormalTok{example.nrm }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.NewAMatrix(example.kw)}
\NormalTok{example.nrm.form\_a\_matrix(example.pedigree)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you know when you load a pedigree file that you want to calculate and
store the NRM you can save a little typing by setting the
\texttt{form\_nrm} option:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{options }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ \{\}}
\NormalTok{options[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}form\_nrm\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{] }\OperatorTok{=} \DecValTok{1}
\NormalTok{...}
\NormalTok{example }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.loadPedigree(options)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you want to inspect the NRM you can use
\texttt{example.nrm.printme()} to print the matrix to the screen, which
is probably not a particularly good idea for large pedigrees.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{[[ }\FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.}   \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.}   \FloatTok{0.5}\NormalTok{ ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.}\NormalTok{  ]]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you want to get the pairwise relationship between two animals you
need to use their renumbered IDs and subtract 1 (because the array is
zero-indexed). For example, if you wanted the coefficient of
relationship between animals \texttt{2} and \texttt{5} (an individual
and its sire) you would use the indices \texttt{1} and \texttt{4}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(example.nrm.nrm[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{][}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{])}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.5}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The NRM is symmetric, which means that \texttt{nrm{[}1{]}{[}4{]}} and
\texttt{nrm\{{[}4{]}{[}1{]}} are identical.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(example.nrm.nrm[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{][}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{])}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.5}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(example.nrm.nrm[}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{][}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{])}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}} \FloatTok{0.5}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can also easily save the NRM to a file for future reference:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{example.nrm.save(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Amatrix.txt\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

You can verify that the contents of \texttt{Amatrix.txt} are:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.0} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25}
\FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.0} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25}
\FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25}
\FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.0} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25}
\FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{1.0} \FloatTok{0.0} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.0}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.0} \FloatTok{1.0} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.0}  \FloatTok{0.5}
\FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.25} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5} \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{0.5}  \FloatTok{1.0}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Information on the endianness and precision of the data in the array are
lost, so this is not a good way to archive data or move data between
machines with different endianness. The data are always written in C
(row-major) order. For better performance you can set the
\texttt{nrm\_format} option to \texttt{binary}, which will use a binary
file format instead of text.

Finally, if you're going to work on the exact same pedigree later you
can load \texttt{Amatrix.txt} and avoid having to recalculate the NRM
entirely:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{example.nrm2 }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_newclasses.NewAMatrix(example.kw)}
\NormalTok{example.nrm2.load(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Amatrix.txt\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{example.nrm2.printme()}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

Since we've loaded \texttt{Amatrix.txt} into a second NRM
(\texttt{nrm2}) attached to our pedigree it's straightforward, if
tedious, to verify that the two NRM contain the same values.

\section{Matings}\label{sec-computing-matings}

There are many questions you might wish to ask that involve matings,
such as, ``What's the minimum-inbreeding mating among this set of
individuals?''. Several routines in \texttt{pyp\_metrics}\ldots{}

Suppose you're considering a mating between animals \texttt{5} (index 4)
and \texttt{14} (index 13), which is a sire-daughter mating. How would
you go about this? You simply call \texttt{pyp\_metrics.mating\_coi()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{print} \StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\CharTok{\textbackslash{}t}\StringTok{Calling mating\_coi() at }\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}} \OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{ ( pyp\_nice\_time() )}
\NormalTok{f }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_metrics.mating\_coi(example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{].animalID,}
\NormalTok{    example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{].animalID,example,}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(f)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

which produces the output:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Calling mating\_coi() at Wed Mar  }\DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{31}\NormalTok{:}\DecValTok{30} \DecValTok{2008}
\FloatTok{0.25}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

We don't need PyPedal to tell us the coefficient of inbreeding of such a
simple mating, but the calculations can be complex for more complicated
cases. While you can do all of the necessary computations ``by hand'',
\texttt{pyp\_metrics.mating\_coi()} takes care of that for you by adding
a new dummy animal to the pedigree with the proposed parents,
calculating the coefficient of inbreeding of that mating, deleting the
dummy animal from the pedigree, and returning the coefficient of
inbreeding.

PyPedal takes things one step further, allowing you to work with groups
of proposed matings at one time using
\texttt{pyp\_metrics.mating\_coi\_group()}. Internally it works much
like \texttt{pyp\_metrics.mating\_coi()}, although instead of passing a
pair of parents you pass a list of proposed matings. The matings are of
the form
\texttt{\textless{}parent1\textgreater{}\_\textless{}parent2\textgreater{}}.
Here's an example in which we're going to consider the following three
matings: \texttt{1} with \texttt{5}, \texttt{1} with \texttt{14}, and
\texttt{5} with \texttt{14}. Note how the matings are formed and
appended to the \texttt{matings} list in a single step.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{matings }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ []}
\NormalTok{matings.append(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\_}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{(example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{].animalID, example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{].animalID))}
\NormalTok{matings.append(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\_}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{(example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{0}\NormalTok{].animalID, example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{].animalID))}
\NormalTok{matings.append(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\_}\SpecialCharTok{\%s}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}}\OperatorTok{\%}\NormalTok{(example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{].animalID, example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{].animalID))}
\NormalTok{fgrp }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_metrics.mating\_coi\_group(matings,example)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}fgrp: \textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, fgrp[}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}matings\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{])}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

That code produces a list of the proposed matings with associated
coefficients of inbreeding. In addition to the \texttt{matings}
dictionary, the \texttt{fgrp} dictionary also contains a dictionary
named \texttt{metadata} which contains summary statistics about the
proposed matings.

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{fgrp:  \{}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1\_5\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}5\_14\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.25}\NormalTok{, }\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}1\_14\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{: }\FloatTok{0.125}\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Relatives}\label{sec-computing-relatives}

PyPedal provides many tools for extracting information about relatives
from a pedigree. Examples include: obtaining lists of the ancestors of
an animal, getting lists of ancestors shared in common by a pair of
animals, listing the descendants of an individual, calculating the
additive genetic relationship between a given pair of animals, and the
creation of ``subpedigrees'' containing only specified animals.

It's easy to obtain a list of an animal's relatives using
\texttt{pyp\_metrics.related\_animals()}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{list\_a }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_metrics.related\_animals(example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{].animalID,example)}
\NormalTok{list\_b }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_metrics.related\_animals(example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{].animalID,example)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

produces a list of each animal's relatives:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{[}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{]}
\NormalTok{[}\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\texttt{pyp\_metrics.common\_ancestors()} is used to get a list of the
common ancestors of two animals, say animals \texttt{5} and \texttt{14}
(remember that this pedigree is renumbered, and Python lists are indexed
from 0, so we need to offset the animal IDs by 1 to get the correct
animal IDs).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{list\_r }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_metrics.common\_ancestors(example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{].animalID,example.pedigree[}\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{].ani}
\NormalTok{malID,example)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(list\_r)}
\NormalTok{\textbackslash{}end\{verbatim\}}
\NormalTok{Results are returned }\ImportTok{as}\NormalTok{ lists:}
\NormalTok{\textbackslash{}begin\{verbatim\}}
\NormalTok{[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

If you've already obtained ancestor lists for a given pair of animals in
which you're interested you can also obtain a list of common ancestors
using Python sets, which avoids performing more calculations than
needed:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ set\_a }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{set}\NormalTok{(list\_a)}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ set\_b }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{set}\NormalTok{(list\_b)}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ set\_c }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ set\_a.intersection(set\_b)}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ set\_c}
\BuiltInTok{set}\NormalTok{([}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{])}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ list\_c }\OperatorTok{=} \BuiltInTok{list}\NormalTok{(set\_c)}
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ list\_c}
\NormalTok{[}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{]}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

A list of descendants can be obtained by calling
\texttt{pyp\_metrics.descendants()}, which returns a dictionary:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ pyp\_metrics.descendants(}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{,example,\{\})}
\NormalTok{\{}\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

However, it's improtant to note that you won't get the answer you expect
unless you have either set the option \texttt{set\_offspring\ =\ 1} or
called \texttt{pyp\_utils.set\_offspring()} after loading the pedigree.
There's also a convenience function,
\texttt{pyp\_metrics.founder\_descendants()}, for handling the special
case of obtaining descendants of all the founders in the pedigree:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\OperatorTok{\textgreater{}\textgreater{}\textgreater{}}\NormalTok{ pyp\_metrics.founder\_descendants(example)}
\NormalTok{\{}\DecValTok{1}\NormalTok{: \{}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{\},}
 \DecValTok{2}\NormalTok{: \{}\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{5}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{\},}
 \DecValTok{3}\NormalTok{: \{}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{\},}
 \DecValTok{4}\NormalTok{: \{}\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{6}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{7}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{8}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{9}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{10}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{11}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{12}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{13}\NormalTok{, }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{: }\DecValTok{14}\NormalTok{\}\}}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

\section{Decomposition and Direct Inverses of Numerator Relationship
Matrices}\label{sec-computing-decomposition}

PyPedal provides routines in the \texttt{pyp\_nrm} module for
decomposing and forming direct inverses of numerator relationship
matrices (NRM). The NRM, \(A\), may be written as \(A=TDT'\), where
\(T\) is a lower triangular matrix and \(D\) is a diagonal matrix
(Henderson 1976; Thompson 1977). \(T\) traces the flow of genes from one
generation to another, while \(D\) provides variances and covariances of
Mendelian sampling. Mrode (1996) presents a more detailed discussion of
this decomposition. The examples in this section all use the pedigree
presented in Table 2.1 of that work, which is contained in the file
\texttt{examples/new\_decompose.ped}:

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\CommentTok{\# Pedigree from Table 2.1 of}
\CommentTok{\# Mrode (1996)}
\DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{2} \DecValTok{0} \DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{3} \DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{2}
\DecValTok{4} \DecValTok{1} \DecValTok{0}
\DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{4} \DecValTok{3}
\DecValTok{6} \DecValTok{5} \DecValTok{2}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

The results presented below may be obtained by running the
\texttt{new\_decompose.py} example program. The
\texttt{pyp\_nrm.a\_decompose()} function takes as its argument a
pedigree object and returns the matrices \(T\) and \(D\). The code

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}Calling a\_decompose()\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}=====================\textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{)}
\NormalTok{D, T  }\OperatorTok{=}\NormalTok{ pyp\_nrm.a\_decompose(example)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}D: \textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, D)}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{()}
\BuiltInTok{print}\NormalTok{(}\StringTok{\textquotesingle{}T: \textquotesingle{}}\NormalTok{, T)}
\end{Highlighting}
\end{Shaded}

produces identical answers to those presented by Mrode (1996) (note that
outputs may have been reformatted slightly to improve readability).

\begin{Shaded}
\begin{Highlighting}[]
\NormalTok{Calling a\_decompose()}
\OperatorTok{=====================}
\NormalTok{D:  [[ }\FloatTok{1.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{     ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{1.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{     ]}
\NormalTok{ [ }\FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.5}      \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}       \FloatTok{0.}\NormalTok{     ]}
